Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. Um... I'm not entirely sure if this is the right place, so I hope it's okay to talk about this. I guess I've just been... well, stressing a bit too much lately? I know it probably sounds really shallow, and I'm sorry to bother you with it, but... I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off.

Um... I really, really try not to let it affect anything. I hate letting people down, and I always want to do a good job, so I try to just push through it. But... I just get so worried that people are looking at how off my face is, and I'd rather just stay home. I know I shouldn't let it get in the way of my life like this, and I really am trying to be better about it. Does that make me sound really irresponsible? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... I mean, when I ask people, they usually tell me they don't see it. And I really, really try to believe them, I do! I don't want to be the kind of person who constantly needs reassurance. 

But... honestly? I feel like they're just being nice to me. Because whenever I see myself in an ID photo, or... or when I have to have my camera on for online classes, it just looks so obviously off. Like my nose and my jaw on the left side are just completely crooked. And even though people say they don't notice, when I'm in the CNC lab, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my face being weird before they even look at the parts I'm machining. 

I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly self-centered, thinking everyone is looking at me. I really try not to let it get to me, but it's just so hard to shake that feeling. Do you... do you think it's normal to feel that way when people tell you everything is fine? I hope I don't sound completely crazy for thinking they can actually see it.

Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. You're the expert, and I trust your judgment way more than my own right now.

Is it... is it bad if I say my perception of it pretty much just stays the same?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face been affecting your relationships with others or your work performance?\n\nPatient: Um... I really, really try not to let it affect anything. I hate letting people down, and I always want to do a good job, so I try to just push through it. But... I guess if I'm being completely honest—and I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for this—it has been getting kind of hard lately. \n\nThere have been a few mornings recently where I just... I couldn't get myself out the door. I ended up missing some of my CNC labs and a couple of my shop shifts. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy if I don't turn things around. I feel so terrible about it, because I promise I'm usually a really hard worker! I just get so stuck looking in the mirror and panicking about my jaw, and before I know it, I've missed my shift. \n\nAnd I guess with other people... well, I actually stopped playing soccer recently. And I stopped volunteering, too. I just get so worried that people are looking at how off my face is, and I'd rather just stay home. I know I shouldn't let it get in the way of my life like this, and I really am trying to be better about it. Does that make me sound really irresponsible? I'm sorry, I know I should be handling all of this much better than I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually perfectly fine and that your concern about it being crooked is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say yes? I know that's probably what I'm supposed to say, and I really, really want to be rational about this. I don't want you to think I'm completely delusional or anything. I know it's probably just me being overly critical.\n\nBut... when I actually look at it, it just feels so real to me. Like, I can see the crookedness so clearly. But I guess... I guess if you're asking, maybe there is a chance it's not as bad as I think it is? Or maybe it is just in my head? I really try to tell myself that it's fine, I promise I do. I just... I struggle to actually believe it. Does that make sense? I'm sorry, I know I should probably have a better perspective on this. I really hope I'm not being too difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see the crookedness in your face if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: Um... I mean, when I ask people, they usually tell me they don't see it. And I really, really try to believe them, I do! I don't want to be the kind of person who constantly needs reassurance. \n\nBut... honestly? I feel like they're just being nice to me. Because whenever I see myself in an ID photo, or... or when I have to have my camera on for online classes, it just looks so obviously off. Like my nose and my jaw on the left side are just completely crooked. And even though people say they don't notice, when I'm in the CNC lab, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my face being weird before they even look at the parts I'm machining. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly self-centered, thinking everyone is looking at me. I really try not to let it get to me, but it's just so hard to shake that feeling. Do you... do you think it's normal to feel that way when people tell you everything is fine? I hope I don't sound completely crazy for thinking they can actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being crooked is a thought that is not based in reality, even if it feels real to you?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm sorry if I'm being frustrating or if I'm just repeating myself, I just want to make sure I'm looking at this the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your perception of your face being crooked stays the same most of the time, but can you think of any situation or event that might make you feel like it's less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Um... less noticeable? Um... wow. If multiple experts said that... I would really, really want to just agree with them. I mean, I'd have to, right? They're the professionals, and I definitely wouldn't want to be the guy arguing with a whole room of surgeons. That would just be disrespectful.

Um... I really, really wish I could say it would. That sounds like such a logical fix, right? Just looking at it from another angle or through someone else's lens. I would love to tell you that doing that makes it all better.

But... honestly? Whenever someone else takes a picture of me, or if I see a video from a friend's phone... I still just see it. I try so hard to look at the whole picture, like... to just step back and see a normal face like you're suggesting. I really do try to force my brain to see it the way they do, because I know they don't see what I see. 

It just... it hasn't really changed anything for me yet. I still zero right in on the asymmetry. I'm sorry, I know I probably sound like a broken record, just shooting down all these good ideas you have. I really don't mean to be so difficult or negative! I want to see what everyone else sees, I promise. 

Do you think... is there a specific way I should be looking at those photos? If there's a technique or an exercise you want me to try with pictures, I'll absolutely do it. I just want to make sure I'm doing this right, and I hope it's okay that I haven't been able to fix it that way yet.

I hope I'm explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your nose and jaw are actually fine, and you're just perceiving them as crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it's possible? I know I shouldn't be so obsessed with it, and I'm really trying not to be. I don't want to sound like I'm completely detached from reality or anything. \n\nBut... when I look in the mirror, it's just so obvious to me. Like, the asymmetry is just right there. But maybe you're right, maybe I am just hyper-focusing on it? I don't know. I really hope I'm not just wasting your time with something that's all in my head. Do you think I'm just overreacting?\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of your face being crooked stay the same all the time, or are there moments when it seems less noticeable to you?\n\nPatient: Um... that's a really good question. I want to say that there are times when I don't notice it, because I know it sounds really extreme to be thinking about it constantly... and I really, really try not to let it distract me when I'm programming or anything. \n\nBut... honestly, I'm not entirely sure? I haven't really noticed a time when it seems less obvious to me. It just always feels like it's right there, staring back at me. I'm sorry, I wish I could give you a better answer. I can definitely try to keep better track of it if that would help you? Is it... is it bad if I say my perception of it pretty much just stays the same?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your face been affecting your relationships with others or your work performance?\n\nPatient: Um... I really, really try not to let it affect anything. I hate letting people down, and I always want to do a good job, so I try to just push through it. But... I guess if I'm being completely honest—and I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for this—it has been getting kind of hard lately. \n\nThere have been a few mornings recently where I just... I couldn't get myself out the door. I ended up missing some of my CNC labs and a couple of my shop shifts. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy if I don't turn things around. I feel so terrible about it, because I promise I'm usually a really hard worker! I just get so stuck looking in the mirror and panicking about my jaw, and before I know it, I've missed my shift. \n\nAnd I guess with other people... well, I actually stopped playing soccer recently. And I stopped volunteering, too. I just get so worried that people are looking at how off my face is, and I'd rather just stay home. I know I shouldn't let it get in the way of my life like this, and I really am trying to be better about it. Does that make me sound really irresponsible? I'm sorry, I know I should be handling all of this much better than I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face is actually perfectly fine and that your concern about it being crooked is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really want to say yes? I know that's probably what I'm supposed to say, and I really, really want to be rational about this. I don't want you to think I'm completely delusional or anything. I know it's probably just me being overly critical.\n\nBut... when I actually look at it, it just feels so real to me. Like, I can see the crookedness so clearly. But I guess... I guess if you're asking, maybe there is a chance it's not as bad as I think it is? Or maybe it is just in my head? I really try to tell myself that it's fine, I promise I do. I just... I struggle to actually believe it. Does that make sense? I'm sorry, I know I should probably have a better perspective on this. I really hope I'm not being too difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see the crookedness in your face if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: Um... I mean, when I ask people, they usually tell me they don't see it. And I really, really try to believe them, I do! I don't want to be the kind of person who constantly needs reassurance. \n\nBut... honestly? I feel like they're just being nice to me. Because whenever I see myself in an ID photo, or... or when I have to have my camera on for online classes, it just looks so obviously off. Like my nose and my jaw on the left side are just completely crooked. And even though people say they don't notice, when I'm in the CNC lab, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my face being weird before they even look at the parts I'm machining. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly self-centered, thinking everyone is looking at me. I really try not to let it get to me, but it's just so hard to shake that feeling. Do you... do you think it's normal to feel that way when people tell you everything is fine? I hope I don't sound completely crazy for thinking they can actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being crooked is a thought that is not based in reality, even if it feels real to you?\n\nPatient: Um... I know we're kind of circling back to this, and I really, really want to give you the answer you're looking for. I guess... yes? It has to be possible, right? If you're saying it is, then I want to believe you. I really don't want to be someone who is just completely out of touch with reality. I promise I'm usually a very logical person—you have to be in my line of work, with all the programming and exact measurements. \n\nIt's just... it's so hard to separate what my eyes are seeing from what might actually be real. But I am trying so hard to accept that maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me and that the crookedness isn't actually there. Is that... is that the kind of thing you mean? I really hope I'm understanding you right. I'm sorry if I'm being frustrating or if I'm just repeating myself, I just want to make sure I'm looking at this the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your perception of your face being crooked stays the same most of the time, but can you think of any situation or event that might make you feel like it's less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Um... less noticeable? I... I really wish I could say yes to that. I know it would probably mean I'm making progress, or that I have some control over it, and I really want to be able to tell you that I do. \n\nBut... honestly? I can't really think of a time when it goes away or feels better. It's just... always there. Like this constant weight. Even when I'm completely focused on programming a machine, or... or when I'm just alone in my room, I still feel it. I know I should probably try harder to find distractions or focus on the positive things, and I promise I will try to do that if you think it would help! It's just... right now, I don't really have a time where it fades. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not a very helpful answer. Does that mean my case is really bad? I hope I'm not a lost cause or anything. I really do want to get better and do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is completely normal and the crookedness you see is not something that anyone else would notice, no matter how closely they looked?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. You're the expert, and I trust your judgment way more than my own right now. \n\nIt's just... it's really hard to wrap my head around the idea that *no one* would notice, no matter how closely they looked. Because when I look in the mirror, it just seems so obvious to me. But... but I know I'm probably just being obsessive, right? I know I shouldn't be staring at it so much. \n\nI guess... yes, it has to be possible that it's completely normal. I'm really trying to open my mind to that. I don't want to be stubborn or refuse to listen to reason. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm still struggling to fully believe it? I'm sorry if I'm being difficult, I really am trying to see it the way you do. Do you think I'm making any sense, or am I just overthinking everything again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still appear crooked to you if multiple doctors and experts in plastic surgery told you that there is no noticeable asymmetry?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If multiple experts said that... I would really, really want to just agree with them. I mean, I'd have to, right? They're the professionals, and I definitely wouldn't want to be the guy arguing with a whole room of surgeons. That would just be disrespectful. \n\nSo I would absolutely try my hardest to accept it. Like I said, I work with CNC machines, so I know you can't argue with exact dimensions. If they showed me the math and the measurements, I would want to trust the data. But... if I'm being completely honest—and I really am trying to be as honest as possible with you—I just don't know for sure if my brain would immediately stop seeing it when I looked in the mirror. \n\nI don't know if the image itself would change for me, even if I knew logically they were right. I feel terrible even admitting that. Does that make me sound really uncooperative or... or delusional? I promise I wouldn't fight them on it, I just... I honestly don't know if my eyes would cooperate right away. Is it bad that I'm not entirely sure? I really hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you saw a video or photo of yourself from someone else's perspective?\n\nPatient: Um... I really, really wish I could say it would. That sounds like such a logical fix, right? Just looking at it from another angle or through someone else's lens. I would love to tell you that doing that makes it all better.\n\nBut... honestly? Whenever someone else takes a picture of me, or if I see a video from a friend's phone... I still just see it. I try so hard to look at the whole picture, like... to just step back and see a normal face like you're suggesting. I really do try to force my brain to see it the way they do, because I know they don't see what I see. \n\nIt just... it hasn't really changed anything for me yet. I still zero right in on the asymmetry. I'm sorry, I know I probably sound like a broken record, just shooting down all these good ideas you have. I really don't mean to be so difficult or negative! I want to see what everyone else sees, I promise. \n\nDo you think... is there a specific way I should be looking at those photos? If there's a technique or an exercise you want me to try with pictures, I'll absolutely do it. I just want to make sure I'm doing this right, and I hope it's okay that I haven't been able to fix it that way yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... a 3D scan? Wow, that... that actually sounds incredible. You know, since I work with CNC programming and CAD software, I look at 3D models all day long. So... so I would really, really want that to work. If you could show me the exact topography, the actual data proving it's symmetrical... I would try so hard to just accept it. I'd want to agree with you and the computer, absolutely. 

But... I guess I just don't know for sure if it would completely change how I see myself in the mirror afterward. I've never had a scan like that done, so I can't say for certain. I'm almost afraid that... well, I feel terrible even admitting this, but I'm afraid I might look at the perfect 3D model and still try to find where the scanner made a mistake? 

Oh gosh, that makes me sound completely irrational, doesn't it? I'm so sorry. I know how computers work, I know the data doesn't lie. I really don't want to be one of those patients who just shoots down every single thing you suggest. I promise I'm trying to be open-minded. I would absolutely be willing to try a scan like that if you think it's a good idea. Do you... do you think an approach like that is something that could actually help me? I'll do whatever you think is best.

I'm sorry if I'm being frustrating or if I'm just repeating myself, I just want to make sure I'm looking at this the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your perception of your face being crooked stays the same most of the time, but can you think of any situation or event that might make you feel like it's less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Um... less noticeable? I... I really wish I could say yes to that. I know it would probably mean I'm making progress, or that I have some control over it, and I really want to be able to tell you that I do. \n\nBut... honestly? I can't really think of a time when it goes away or feels better. It's just... always there. Like this constant weight. Even when I'm completely focused on programming a machine, or... or when I'm just alone in my room, I still feel it. I know I should probably try harder to find distractions or focus on the positive things, and I promise I will try to do that if you think it would help! It's just... right now, I don't really have a time where it fades. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not a very helpful answer. Does that mean my case is really bad? I hope I'm not a lost cause or anything. I really do want to get better and do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is completely normal and the crookedness you see is not something that anyone else would notice, no matter how closely they looked?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. You're the expert, and I trust your judgment way more than my own right now. \n\nIt's just... it's really hard to wrap my head around the idea that *no one* would notice, no matter how closely they looked. Because when I look in the mirror, it just seems so obvious to me. But... but I know I'm probably just being obsessive, right? I know I shouldn't be staring at it so much. \n\nI guess... yes, it has to be possible that it's completely normal. I'm really trying to open my mind to that. I don't want to be stubborn or refuse to listen to reason. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm still struggling to fully believe it? I'm sorry if I'm being difficult, I really am trying to see it the way you do. Do you think I'm making any sense, or am I just overthinking everything again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still appear crooked to you if multiple doctors and experts in plastic surgery told you that there is no noticeable asymmetry?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If multiple experts said that... I would really, really want to just agree with them. I mean, I'd have to, right? They're the professionals, and I definitely wouldn't want to be the guy arguing with a whole room of surgeons. That would just be disrespectful. \n\nSo I would absolutely try my hardest to accept it. Like I said, I work with CNC machines, so I know you can't argue with exact dimensions. If they showed me the math and the measurements, I would want to trust the data. Even when I'm completely focused on programming a machine, or... or when I'm just alone in my room, I still feel it. It's just... right now, I don't really have a time where it fades. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not a very helpful answer. Does that mean my case is really bad? I hope I'm not a lost cause or anything. I really do want to get better and do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is completely normal and the crookedness you see is not something that anyone else would notice, no matter how closely they looked?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. You're the expert, and I trust your judgment way more than my own right now. \n\nIt's just... it's really hard to wrap my head around the idea that *no one* would notice, no matter how closely they looked. Because when I look in the mirror, it just seems so obvious to me. But... but I know I'm probably just being obsessive, right? I know I shouldn't be staring at it so much. \n\nI guess... yes, it has to be possible that it's completely normal. I'm really trying to open my mind to that. I don't want to be stubborn or refuse to listen to reason. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm still struggling to fully believe it? I'm sorry if I'm being difficult, I really am trying to see it the way you do. Do you think I'm making any sense, or am I just overthinking everything again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still appear crooked to you if multiple doctors and experts in plastic surgery told you that there is no noticeable asymmetry?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If multiple experts said that... I would really, really want to just agree with them. I mean, I'd have to, right? Like I said, I work with CNC machines, so I know you can't argue with exact dimensions. If they showed me the math and the measurements, I would want to trust the data. But... if I'm being completely honest—and I really am trying to be as honest as possible with you—I just don't know for sure if my brain would immediately stop seeing it when I looked in the mirror. \n\nI don't know if the image itself would change for me, even if I knew logically they were right. I feel terrible even admitting that. Does that make me sound really uncooperative or... or delusional? I promise I wouldn't fight them on it, I just... I honestly don't know if my eyes would cooperate right away. Is it bad that I'm not entirely sure? I really hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you saw a video or photo of yourself from someone else's perspective?\n\nPatient: Um... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... wow, that's a really interesting question. I... I really wish I could give you a solid yes or no on that. 

I want to say that seeing it on someone else would make me realize it's fine. I definitely don't judge other people's faces the way I judge my own, so maybe... maybe I wouldn't see the crookedness on them? Or maybe I'd be more forgiving of it? I would really hope so. 

But if it was exactly, perfectly identical to my face... I honestly just don't know. I've never been in a situation like that, obviously, so I can't say for sure how my eyes would process it. I'm so sorry, I feel like I'm not being very helpful with these hypothetical questions. I really want to give you a good, clear answer, I just... I'm not entirely sure what my brain would do in that scenario. 

Is... is there a typical way people usually answer that? Like, what you'd expect to hear? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information so you can do your job. I really hope it's okay that I'm just completely unsure about this one.

If you're saying it is, then I want to believe you. I really don't want to be someone who is just completely out of touch with reality. I promise I'm usually a very logical person—you have to be in my line of work, with all the programming and exact measurements. \n\nIt's just... it's so hard to separate what my eyes are seeing from what might actually be real. But I am trying so hard to accept that maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me and that the crookedness isn't actually there. Is that... is that the kind of thing you mean? I really hope I'm understanding you right. I'm sorry if I'm being frustrating or if I'm just repeating myself, I just want to make sure I'm looking at this the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your perception of your face being crooked stays the same most of the time, but can you think of any situation or event that might make you feel like it's less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Um... less noticeable? I... I really wish I could say yes to that. I know it would probably mean I'm making progress, or that I have some control over it, and I really want to be able to tell you that I do. \n\nBut... honestly? Even when I'm completely focused on programming a machine, or... or when I'm just alone in my room, I still feel it. It's just... right now, I don't really have a time where it fades. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not a very helpful answer. Does that mean my case is really bad? I hope I'm not a lost cause or anything. I really do want to get better and do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is completely normal and the crookedness you see is not something that anyone else would notice, no matter how closely they looked?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. I feel terrible even admitting that. Does that make me sound really uncooperative or... or delusional? I promise I wouldn't fight them on it, I just... I honestly don't know if my eyes would cooperate right away. Is it bad that I'm not entirely sure? I really hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you saw a video or photo of yourself from someone else's perspective?\n\nPatient: Um... I really, really wish I could say it would. That sounds like such a logical fix, right? Just looking at it from another angle or through someone else's lens. I guess if you're asking, maybe there is a chance it's not as bad as I think it is? Or maybe it is just in my head? I really try to tell myself that it's fine, I promise I do. I just... I struggle to actually believe it. Does that make sense? I'm sorry, I know I should probably have a better perspective on this. I really hope I'm not being too difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else would be able to see the crookedness in your face if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: Um... I mean, when I ask people, they usually tell me they don't see it. And I really, really try to believe them, I do! I don't want to be the kind of person who constantly needs reassurance. \n\nBut... honestly? I feel like they're just being nice to me. Because whenever I see myself in an ID photo, or... or when I have to have my camera on for online classes, it just looks so obviously off. Like my nose and my jaw on the left side are just completely crooked. And even though people say they don't notice, when I'm in the CNC lab, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my face being weird before they even look at the parts I'm machining. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds incredibly self-centered, thinking everyone is looking at me. I really try not to let it get to me, but it's just so hard to shake that feeling. Do you... do you think it's normal to feel that way when people tell you everything is fine? I hope I don't sound completely crazy for thinking they can actually see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being crooked is a thought that is not based in reality, even if it feels real to you?\n\nPatient: Um... I know we're kind of circling back to this, and I really, really want to give you the answer you're looking for. I guess... yes? It has to be possible, right? If you're saying it is, then I want to believe you. I really don't want to be someone who is just completely out of touch with reality. I promise I'm usually a very logical person—you have to be in my line of work, with all the programming and exact measurements. \n\nIt's just... it's so hard to separate what my eyes are seeing from what might actually be real. But I am trying so hard to accept that maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me and that the crookedness isn't actually there. Is that... is that the kind of thing you mean? I really hope I'm understanding you right. I'm sorry if I'm being frustrating or if I'm just repeating myself, I just want to make sure I'm looking at this the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your perception of your face being crooked stays the same most of the time, but can you think of any situation or event that might make you feel like it's less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Um... less noticeable? I... I really wish I could say yes to that. I know it would probably mean I'm making progress, or that I have some control over it, and I really want to be able to tell you that I do. \n\nBut... honestly? I can't really think of a time when it goes away or feels better. It's just... always there. Like this constant weight. Even when I'm completely focused on programming a machine, or... or when I'm just alone in my room, I still feel it. I know I should probably try harder to find distractions or focus on the positive things, and I promise I will try to do that if you think it would help! It's just... right now, I don't really have a time where it fades. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not a very helpful answer. Does that mean my case is really bad? I hope I'm not a lost cause or anything. I really do want to get better and do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face is completely normal and the crookedness you see is not something that anyone else would notice, no matter how closely they looked?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. Completely normal? I... I really want to say yes. I mean, if you're telling me that's a possibility, then I absolutely want to believe you. You're the expert, and I trust your judgment way more than my own right now. \n\nIt's just... it's really hard to wrap my head around the idea that *no one* would notice, no matter how closely they looked. Because when I look in the mirror, it just seems so obvious to me. But... but I know I'm probably just being obsessive, right? I know I shouldn't be staring at it so much. \n\nI guess... yes, it has to be possible that it's completely normal. I'm really trying to open my mind to that. I don't want to be stubborn or refuse to listen to reason. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm still struggling to fully believe it? I'm sorry if I'm being difficult, I really am trying to see it the way you do. Do you think I'm making any sense, or am I just overthinking everything again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still appear crooked to you if multiple doctors and experts in plastic surgery told you that there is no noticeable asymmetry?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If multiple experts said that... I would really, really want to just agree with them. I mean, I'd have to, right? They're the professionals, and I definitely wouldn't want to be the guy arguing with a whole room of surgeons. That would just be disrespectful. \n\nSo I would absolutely try my hardest to accept it. Like I said, I work with CNC machines, so I know you can't argue with exact dimensions. If they showed me the math and the measurements, I would want to trust the data. But... if I'm being completely honest—and I really am trying to be as honest as possible with you—I just don't know for sure if my brain would immediately stop seeing it when I looked in the mirror. \n\nI don't know if the image itself would change for me, even if I knew logically they were right. I feel terrible even admitting that. Does that make me sound really uncooperative or... or delusional? I promise I wouldn't fight them on it, I just... I honestly don't know if my eyes would cooperate right away. Is it bad that I'm not entirely sure? I really hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you saw a video or photo of yourself from someone else's perspective?\n\nPatient: Um... I really, really wish I could say it would. That sounds like such a logical fix, right? Just looking at it from another angle or through someone else's lens. I would love to tell you that doing that makes it all better.\n\nBut... honestly? Whenever someone else takes a picture of me, or if I see a video from a friend's phone... I still just see it. I try so hard to look at the whole picture, like... to just step back and see a normal face like you're suggesting. I really do try to force my brain to see it the way they do, because I know they don't see what I see. \n\nIt just... it hasn't really changed anything for me yet. I still zero right in on the asymmetry. I'm sorry, I know I probably sound like a broken record, just shooting down all these good ideas you have. I really don't mean to be so difficult or negative! I want to see what everyone else sees, I promise. \n\nDo you think... is there a specific way I should be looking at those photos? If there's a technique or an exercise you want me to try with pictures, I'll absolutely do it. I just want to make sure I'm doing this right, and I hope it's okay that I haven't been able to fix it that way yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you were shown a 3D scan or model of your face that confirmed its symmetry?\n\nPatient: Um... a 3D scan? Wow, that... that actually sounds incredible. You know, since I work with CNC programming and CAD software, I look at 3D models all day long. So... so I would really, really want that to work. If you could show me the exact topography, the actual data proving it's symmetrical... I would try so hard to just accept it. I'd want to agree with you and the computer, absolutely. \n\nBut... I guess I just don't know for sure if it would completely change how I see myself in the mirror afterward. I've never had a scan like that done, so I can't say for certain. I'm almost afraid that... well, I feel terrible even admitting this, but I'm afraid I might look at the perfect 3D model and still try to find where the scanner made a mistake? \n\nOh gosh, that makes me sound completely irrational, doesn't it? I'm so sorry. I know how computers work, I know the data doesn't lie. I really don't want to be one of those patients who just shoots down every single thing you suggest. I promise I'm trying to be open-minded. I would absolutely be willing to try a scan like that if you think it's a good idea. Do you... do you think an approach like that is something that could actually help me? I'll do whatever you think is best.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked would change if you were diagnosed with a mental health condition that involves distorted self-image?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If I was diagnosed with something like that? I mean... I would really, really want to believe it. Like, if you told me right now that this was all just... just a glitch in my brain, a condition or something... I would want to accept that completely. It would actually be kind of a relief, you know? To know I'm not just being vain or... or crazy. \n\nI would try so hard to just trust your diagnosis. You're the doctor, and I definitely don't want to be the kind of person who argues with a professional who's just trying to help. But... I guess I'm just not totally sure if putting a name to it would immediately make the crookedness disappear when I look in the mirror. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds so stubborn. I really don't mean to be! I just... I don't know if knowing the label would change the actual picture my eyes see, even if it changes how I understand it logically. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being too difficult. I would absolutely listen to whatever you tell me, I just... I guess I don't have a perfect answer for how my brain would react. Is it okay that I don't know for sure?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would appear crooked to you even if you were shown a mirror reflection of someone else's face that was identical to yours?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's a really interesting question. I... I really wish I could give you a solid yes or no on that. \n\nI want to say that seeing it on someone else would make me realize it's fine. I definitely don't judge other people's faces the way I judge my own, so maybe... maybe I wouldn't see the crookedness on them? Or maybe I'd be more forgiving of it? I would really hope so. \n\nBut if it was exactly, perfectly identical to my face... I honestly just don't know. I've never been in a situation like that, obviously, so I can't say for sure how my eyes would process it. I'm so sorry, I feel like I'm not being very helpful with these hypothetical questions. I really want to give you a good, clear answer, I just... I'm not entirely sure what my brain would do in that scenario. \n\nIs... is there a typical way people usually answer that? Like, what you'd expect to hear? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information so you can do your job. I really hope it's okay that I'm just completely unsure about this one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would appear crooked to you even if multiple mental health professionals agreed that your concern about its appearance is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Um... wow. If multiple professionals all agreed? I... I really want to say yes, that would fix it. I mean, if a whole team of experts like you sat me down and told me it wasn't real... I would try so, so hard to believe you all. I really respect doctors and I know you guys train for years to understand these things. \n\nSo I would want to just accept it and move on. I really would. I wouldn't want to be the difficult patient who thinks he knows better than a room full of experts. But... I guess I just don't know if hearing those words would actually change what my eyes physically see when I look in the mirror. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel terrible saying that. It makes me sound so arrogant, like I wouldn't listen to professionals. I promise I would listen! I just... I honestly don't know if my brain would let me see it differently, even if I logically knew you were all right. I hope that's an okay answer? I'm really trying to be honest with you, I just... I don't know for sure how I'd react. Is it normal to feel this unsure about everything?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... a room completely without mirrors? That's... wow, I'm trying to imagine that. I guess... well, obviously I wouldn't be able to physically see my reflection, so my eyes wouldn't be looking at it. I would really try to use that time to just relax and not worry about it. Like when I'm at work setting up the CNC machines, I try to just focus on the tools and the programming and be a good, productive employee, you know? 

But... even without a mirror or a window to look into, I think my brain still kind of... draws it for me? Like I can still almost feel how uneven it is, and I just picture it in my head. So it still feels crooked to me, even if I'm not looking right at a reflection of it. 

I mean, I really wish I could just say no, it wouldn't bother me at all if the mirrors were gone. I know that's probably the healthier answer, and I really don't want you to think I'm completely lost in my own head all the time. I try really hard not to be! But I just don't know how to turn off that mental picture. 

Does... does that make sense for what you're asking? I hope I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. I know you're asking about what I'd actually see, and I don't mean to overcomplicate it. I'm really trying my best to be a good patient for you.

I couldn't get myself out the door. I ended up missing some of my CNC labs and a couple of my shop shifts. I feel so terrible about it, because I promise I'm usually a really hard worker! I just get so stuck looking in the mirror and panicking about my jaw, and before I know it, I've missed my shift. \n\nAnd I guess with other people... well, I actually stopped playing soccer recently. I really try not to let it get to me, but it's just so hard to shake that feeling. Do you... do you think it's normal to feel that way when people tell you everything is fine? Um... hypnotized? Wow, that's an interesting question. I've never been hypnotized before, but I know it can be really powerful for some people. Um... a digitally altered photo? Like, if someone used Photoshop to make it completely even? That's... wow, that's really clever. I hadn't thought of that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow. A medical certificate... from an actual plastic surgeon? 

I mean, I have so much respect for doctors. You guys go to school for so long and know exactly what you're doing, you know? I might just assume there was something wrong with the lighting, or... or that I'm just completely broken for seeing it when a professional says it isn't there. 

I'm sorry, I really just don't know for sure how I'd react. I hope it's okay that I don't have a solid answer for that one. Does that make sense? I really don't want you to think I'm the kind of person who just ignores medical advice, I promise I'm not.
